Counties of Croatia

Croatia was first divided into counties in the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages).[^cite_note-Medieval-Counties-201] The divisions changed over time to reflect losses of territory to Ottoman conquest and subsequent liberation of the same territory, changes of the political status of Dalmatia, Dubrovnik, and [Istria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istria). The traditional division of the country into counties was abolished in the 1920s when the Kingdom of Serbs, Croats and Slovenes and the subsequent Kingdom of Yugoslavia introduced [oblasts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oblast) and [banovinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subdivisions_of_the_Kingdom_of_Yugoslavia) respectively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrucht2005429-202]&#x20;

Communist-ruled Croatia, as a constituent part of post-World War II Yugoslavia, abolished earlier divisions and introduced municipalities, subdividing Croatia into approximately one hundred municipalities. Counties were reintroduced in 1992 legislation, significantly altered in terms of territory relative to the pre-1920s subdivisions. In 1918, the [Transleithanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transleithania) part was divided into eight counties with their seats in [Bjelovar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bjelovar), [Gospić](923489.md), [Ogulin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ogulin), [Osijek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osijek), [Požega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Po%C5%BEega,_Croatia), [Varaždin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vara%C5%BEdin), Vukovar, and Zagreb.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiondich200011-203][^cite_note-CountiesAct1992-204]&#x20;

Since 1992, Croatia has been divided into [20 counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counties_of_Croatia) and the [capital city of Zagreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_city_of_Zagreb), the latter having the dual authority and legal status of a county and a city. County borders changed in some instances, last revised in 2006. The counties subdivide into [127 cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Croatia) and [429 municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_Croatia).[^cite_note-CountiesAct2006-205] [Nomenclature of Territorial Units for Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomenclature_of_Territorial_Units_for_Statistics) (NUTS) [division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_Croatia) is performed in several tiers. NUTS 1 level considers the entire country in a single unit; three NUTS 2 regions come below that. Those are Northwest Croatia, Central and Eastern (Pannonian) Croatia, and Adriatic Croatia. The latter encompasses the counties along the Adriatic coast. Northwest Croatia includes Koprivnica-Križevci, Krapina-Zagorje, Međimurje, Varaždin, the city of Zagreb, and Zagreb counties and the Central and Eastern (Pannonian) Croatia includes the remaining areas—Bjelovar-Bilogora, Brod-Posavina, Karlovac, Osijek-Baranja, Požega-Slavonia, Sisak-Moslavina, Virovitica-Podravina, and Vukovar-Syrmia counties. Individual counties and the city of Zagreb also represent NUTS 3 level subdivision units in Croatia. The NUTS [local administrative unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_division) (LAU) divisions are two-tiered. LAU 1 divisions match the counties and the city of Zagreb in effect making those the same as NUTS 3 units, while LAU 2 subdivisions correspond to cities and municipalities.[^cite_note-206]&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Croatia)



The capital city of [Zagreb](55906.md) is the nation's primary economic and financial centre.&#x20;

Croatia has a [highly developed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy).[^cite_note-\:0-207][^cite_note-208][^cite_note-World_Bank_Country_Classification-209] It is one of the [largest economies in Southeast Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)#Table) by nominal [gross domestic product](12594.md) (GDP).[^cite_note-GDP_IMF-210][^cite_note-211][^cite_note-IMF-212] It maintains a similarly [high regional GDP-per-capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)_per_capita).[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214] It is an [open economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_economy) with accommodative foreign policy, highly dependent on international trade in Europe. Within Croatia, economic development [varies among its counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Croatian_counties_by_GDP), with strongest growth in [Central Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Croatia) and its financial centre, Zagreb. It has a [very high level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Croatian_counties_by_Human_Development_Index) of human development,[^cite_note-215] low levels of [income inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gini_coefficient), and a high [quality of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quality_of_life).[^cite_note-216] Croatia's [labor market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_economics) has been perennially inefficient, with an underdeveloped investment climate and an ineffective [corporate and income tax system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Croatia).[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218]&#x20;



[Rimac Automobili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rimac_Automobili), one of Croatia's major [automotive manufacturers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automotive_industry_in_Croatia).

Croatia's economic history is closely linked to its historic [nation-building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation-building) efforts. Its pre-industrial economy leveraged the [country's geography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Croatia) and natural resources to guide agricultural growth. The 1800s saw a shipbuilding boom, railroading, and widespread industrialization. During the 1900s, Croatia entered into a [planned economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy) (with [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism)) in 1941 and a [command economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy#Command_economy) (with [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism)) during [World War II](32927.md).[^cite_note-DOS-219] It experienced rapid urbanization in the 1950s and [decentralized](49139.md) in 1965, diversifying its economy before the [independence of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Croatia) in 1990.[^cite_note-DOS-219] The [Croatian War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_War_of_Independence) (1991–95) curbed [21–25% of wartime GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_War_of_Independence#Impact_and_aftermath), leaving behind a developing [transition economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transition_economy).[^cite_note-DOS-219]&#x20;

The modern Croatian economy is considered [high-income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-income), dominated by its [tertiary service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector_of_the_economy) and industry sectors which account for 70% of GDP.[^cite_note-\:27-220][^cite_note-\:28-221] Croatia is an [emerging energy power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Croatia) in Europe, with strategic investments in liquefied natural gas ([LNG](832128.md)), [geothermal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geothermal_energy) networks, and electric transport.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223] It supports regional economic activity via transportation networks along the [Adriatic Sea](23275478.md) and throughout [Pan-European corridors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-European_corridors). Croatia has free-trade agreements with many world nations and is a part of the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) (2000) and the [EEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Economic_Area) (2013).[^cite_note-\:17-224][^cite_note-\:21-225] Croatia's largest [trading partner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_trade) was the rest of the European Union, led by Germany, Italy, and Slovenia.[^cite_note-226] The nation has an [emerging defence sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_industry_of_Croatia), with major manufacturers including [HS Produkt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HS_Produkt) and [Đuro Đaković](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%90uro_%C4%90akovi%C4%87_\(company\)), among others.[^cite_note-vojna_ind-227][^cite_note-\:4-228]&#x20;

### Tourism

Main articles: [Tourism in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Croatia) and [Croatian National Tourist Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_National_Tourist_Board)



[Dubrovnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dubrovnik) is Croatia's most visited and most popular destination.

Tourism is a major industry and economic sector.[^cite_note-229] It has historically represented a large component the country's economic output (GDP), routinely reaching 10% to 15% of total GDP.[^cite_note-\:27-220][^cite_note-230] Croatia is deeply integrated with the European Union (EU), contributing to overall international tourism in [Southeast Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Europe). Tourism is concentrated along the [Adriatic coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adriatic_Croatia) and is strongly seasonal, peaking in July and August.[^cite_note-231] The most frequented cities are [Dubrovnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dubrovnik), [Rovinj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rovinj), [Zagreb](55906.md), [Split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split,_Croatia), [Poreč](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pore%C4%8D), [Umag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umag), and [Zadar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zadar), respectively.[^cite_note-232]&#x20;



[Zlatni Rat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zlatni_Rat) beach on the Island of [Brač](226332.md)

The history of tourism in Croatia dates back to its time as part of [Austria-Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria-Hungary) when wealthy aristocrats would converge to the sea.[^cite_note-baskar-233] Tourism expanded throughout the 1960s to the 1980s before the [independence of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Croatia) in 1990 curbed tourism until the late-1990s.[^cite_note-springer-234] The 2000s saw a significant resurgence of Croatian tourism as it underwent nation-building with a particular emphasis on tourism revenue. By the late-2000s, Croatia became one of the most visited tourist destinations in the [Mediterranean](19006.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia425-235] A total of 20.2 million tourists visited Croatia in 2024.[^cite_note-htz.hr2021-236]&#x20;

Eight areas in the country have been designated national parks and eleven as [nature parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_protected_areas_of_Croatia#Nature_parks).[^cite_note-237] There are [ten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Croatia) [World Heritage Sites](44940.md) and 116 [Blue Flag beaches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Flag_beach) across the country.[^cite_note-croatia_unesco-238][^cite_note-239] Factors of tourist interest are mainly [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Croatia), [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_cuisine), [history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Croatia), fashion, [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Croatia), art, religious sites and routes, nature, maritime access, and nightlife. Tourism has been partially supported by the Croatian film and television industries due to on-location filming. Inflation and [overtourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overtourism) has led to increased travel regulations and tourist costs since 2024.[^cite_note-240] [Eurostat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostat) estimated that nearly 55% of EU tourist accommodation is between Croatia (117,000) and neighboring [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Italy) (230,000).[^cite_note-241] In 2025, Croatia was the ninth-most-visited state in the EU and third-most-visited in [Southern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe).[^cite_note-242]&#x20;

### Infrastructure

See also: [Transport in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Croatia) and [List of tallest buildings in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Croatia)

#### Motorways



[Pelješac Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelje%C5%A1ac_Bridge) connects the peninsula of [Pelješac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelje%C5%A1ac) and through it the southernmost part, including [Dubrovnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dubrovnik), with the [Croatian mainland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatia_proper).

The [motorway network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorways_in_Croatia) was largely built in the late 1990s and the 2000s. As of December 2020, Croatia had completed 1,313.8 kilometres (816.4 miles) of motorways, connecting Zagreb to other regions and following various [European routes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_E-road_network) and four [Pan-European corridors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-European_corridors).[^cite_note-Helsinki-1997-243][^cite_note-NN-Motorways2009-244][^cite_note-245] The busiest motorways are the [A1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_\(Croatia\)), connecting Zagreb to Split and the [A3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A3_\(Croatia\)), passing east to west through northwest Croatia and Slavonia.[^cite_note-HC-promet-246]&#x20;

A widespread network of [state roads in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_roads_in_Croatia) acts as motorway [feeder roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feeder_line_\(network\)) while connecting major settlements. The high quality and safety levels of the Croatian motorway network were tested and confirmed by EuroTAP and EuroTest programmes.[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248]&#x20;

The construction of the 2.4-kilometre-long [Pelješac Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelje%C5%A1ac_Bridge) connected the two-halves of Dubrovnik-Neretva County and shortened the route to the [Pelješac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelje%C5%A1ac) peninsula and the islands of [Korčula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kor%C4%8Dula) and [Lastovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lastovo) by more than 32 km. The construction of the bridge started in July 2018 after Croatian road operator [Hrvatske ceste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hrvatske_ceste) (HC) signed a 2.08 billion [kuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_kuna) deal for the works with a Chinese consortium led by [China Road and Bridge Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Road_and_Bridge_Corporation) (CRBC). The project was co-financed by the European Union with €357 million. The bridge was finished in July 2022, opening that same month.[^cite_note-249]&#x20;

#### Railways

Main article: [Rail transport in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Croatia)

Further information: [List of railways in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_railways_in_Croatia)



Local train to between [Zagreb](55906.md) and [Varaždin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vara%C5%BEdin)



[HŽ series 6112](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C5%BD_series_6112) manufactured by the Croatian company [Končar Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kon%C4%8Dar_Group)



[Zagreb Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagreb_Airport), busiest in the country



[Rijeka Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rijeka)

Croatia has an [extensive rail network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_railways_in_Croatia) spanning 2,604 kilometres (1,618 miles), including 984 kilometres (611 miles) of electrified railways and 254 kilometres (158 miles) of double track railways.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia350-250] The most significant railways in Croatia are within the Pan-European transport corridors Vb and X connecting [Rijeka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rijeka) to [Budapest](36787.md) and Ljubljana to Belgrade, both via [Zagreb](55906.md).[^cite_note-Helsinki-1997-243] In 2024, the [European Investment Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Investment_Bank) committed €400 million to support the revitalization of the railway system, focusing on green and digital transformations.[^cite_note-251]\[^cite_note-[www.koncar.hr-252](http://www.koncar.hr-252/)] In July 2024, a significant agreement was signed for the acquisition of six [electro-diesel multiple units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electro-diesel_multiple_unit) (EDMUs) to improve connectivity between [Split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split,_Croatia) and Zagreb. Valued at €57.3 million and financed through an EIB loan, this project is part of the broader initiative to modernize Croatia's railway infrastructure.\[^cite_note-[www.koncar.hr-252](http://www.koncar.hr-252/)]&#x20;

#### Aviation

Main article: [List of airports in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Croatia)

Further information: [List of the busiest airports in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_Croatia) and [List of Croatian airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Croatian_airlines)

[Airports in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Croatia) are located and serves [Dubrovnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dubrovnik_Airport), [Osijek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osijek_Airport), [Pula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pula_Airport), [Rijeka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rijeka_Airport), [Split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split_Airport), [Zadar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zadar_Airport), and [Zagreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagreb_International_Airport).[^cite_note-MMPI-Airports-253] The largest and busiest is [Franjo Tuđman Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagreb_Airport) in Zagreb, followed by [Saint Jerome Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split_Airport) in Split, [Ruđer Bošković Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dubrovnik_Airport) in Dubrovnik and [Zadar Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zadar_Airport) in Zadar, with annual passenger numbers exceeding one million respectively.[^cite_note-254] [Croatian airports in terms of passenger arrivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_Croatia) recorded more than 10 million tourists in 2018, 11 million in 2019 and 2023, 13 million in 2024. Airports in Croatia welcomed more than 14 million passengers in 2025.[^cite_note-255]&#x20;

As of 2024, Croatian [flag-carrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag-carrier) [Croatia Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatia_Airlines) expanded its international network by introducing direct flights from [Zagreb](55906.md) to [Tirana](31075.md), Berlin, and [Stockholm](26741.md).[^cite_note-256] Additionally, the airline launched services connecting [Hamburg](13467.md) to Zagreb starting 1 July 2024.[^cite_note-257] In July 2024, the airline took delivery of its first [Airbus A220](1567474.md)-300 aircraft, marking the beginning of a transition to a more modern and efficient fleet.[^cite_note-www\.airbus.com_2024-258][^cite_note-aircraft.airbus.com_2024-259] This initiative includes the acquisition of 13 A220-300 and two [A220-100](1567474.md) aircraft, aiming to enhance operational efficiency with 25% lower fuel consumption and reduced emissions.[^cite_note-www\.airbus.com_2024-258][^cite_note-aircraft.airbus.com_2024-259] Other airlines operating and providing private flight services in Croatia include [ETF Airways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ETF_Airways), [Fly Air41 Airways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fly_Air41_Airways), [Trade Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_Air), Air Pannonia, and Jung Sky.[^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263]&#x20;

#### Ports

The busiest cargo seaport is the [Port of Rijeka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rijeka). The busiest passenger ports are [Port of Split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Split), [Port of Dubrovnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gru%C5%BE) and Zadar.[^cite_note-WB-Rijeka-264][^cite_note-MMPI-RL-plan-265] [Port of Ploče](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Plo%C4%8De) serves as Croatia’s second-largest cargo seaport and functions as the primary maritime gateway for the economic hinterland of neighboring Bosnia and Herzegovina.[^cite_note-266] [Jadrolinija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jadrolinija) is the largest and primary [maritime transport](28769.md) and [ferry](50771.md) company in Croatia, connecting Croatian islands with Croatian mainland. In addition to its domestic coastal routes, Jadrolinija provides international passenger services across the [Adriatic Sea](23275478.md) to the Italian ports of [Bari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bari) and [Ancona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancona).[^cite_note-267] Many minor ports serve ferries connecting numerous islands and coastal cities with ferry lines.[^cite_note-AOLPP-Rijeka-268] The largest [river port](1341936.md) is [port of Vukovar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Vukovar), located on the [Danube](21209639.md) in [Vukovar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vukovar), representing the nation's outlet to the Pan-European transport corridor VII.[^cite_note-Helsinki-1997-243][^cite_note-MMPI-River-Navigation-269]&#x20;

#### Energy

See also: [Energy in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Croatia)



Poštak Wind Farm near [Gračac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gra%C4%8Dac), [Zadar County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zadar_County)

610 kilometres (380 miles) of crude oil pipelines serve Croatia, connecting the [Rijeka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rijeka) oil terminal with refineries in Rijeka and [Sisak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sisak), and several transhipment terminals. The system has a capacity of 20 million tonnes per year.[^cite_note-JANAF-system-270] The natural gas transportation system comprises 2,113 kilometres (1,313 miles) of trunk and regional pipelines, and more than 300 associated structures, connecting production rigs, the Okoli natural gas storage facility, 27 end-users and 37 distribution systems.[^cite_note-Plinacro-system-271] Croatia also plays an important role in regional energy security. The floating [liquefied natural gas](832128.md) import terminal off [Krk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krk) island [LNG Hrvatska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LNG_Hrvatska) commenced operations on 1 January 2021, positioning Croatia as a regional energy leader and contributing to diversification of Europe's energy supply.[^cite_note-\:5-272][^cite_note-\:6-273]&#x20;

In 2010, Croatian energy production covered 85% of nationwide natural gas and 19% of oil demand.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2010_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia298–302-274] In 2016, Croatia's primary energy production involved natural gas (24.8%), hydropower (28.3%), crude oil (13.6%), fuelwood (27.6%), and heat pumps and other renewable energy sources (5.7%).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia307-275] In 2017, net total electrical power production reached 11,543 GWh, while it imported 12,157 GWh or about 40% of its electric power energy needs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia304-276] Croatia co-owns 50% of the [Krško Nuclear Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kr%C5%A1ko_Nuclear_Power_Plant) with neighboring [Slovenia](27338.md).[^cite_note-EUB-NEK-277] This power plant supplies a large part of Croatian imports and provides 15% of the nation's electricity.[^cite_note-EUB-NEK-277] According to Eurostat in 2026, Croatia has the highest quantity of water resources per capita in the EU (30,000 m<sup>3</sup>).[^cite_note-278]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Croatia) and [Croats](69801.md)

See also: [List of cities and towns in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Croatia)

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Croats](69801.md) |  | 91.6% |
| [Serbs](59512.md) |  | 3.2% |
| [Other](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Croatia) |  | 5.2% |
| 2021 Croatian Census[^cite_note-Census_2021-5] |  |  |



Ethnic structure of Croatia in 2021.

With an estimated population of 3.87 million in 2024, Croatia [ranks 128th by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_population) in the world.[^cite_note-279] Its 2018 [population density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_and_dependent_territories_by_population_density) was 72.9 inhabitants per square kilometre, making Croatia one of the more sparsely populated European countries.[^cite_note-who0-280] The overall [life expectancy in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy) at birth was 77.7 years in 2024.[^cite_note-cia-281]&#x20;

The [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) of 1.46 children per mother, is one of the [lowest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_and_dependent_territories_by_fertility_rate), far below the replacement rate of 2.1; it remains considerably below the high of 6.18 children rate in 1885.[^cite_note-cia-281][^cite_note-282] Croatia's [death rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_rate) has continuously exceeded its [birth rate](515898.md) since 1998.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia113-283] Croatia subsequently has one of the world's oldest populations, with an average age of 45.1 years.[^cite_note-CIA-150] The population rose steadily from 2.1 million in 1857 until 1991, when it peaked at 4.7 million, with the exceptions of censuses taken in 1921 and 1948, i.e., following the world wars.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia107-284] The natural growth rate is negative[^cite_note-CIA-150] with the [demographic transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographic_transition) completed in the 1970s.[^cite_note-285] In recent years, the Croatian government has been pressured to increase permit quotas for foreign workers, reaching an all-time high of 68.100 in 2019.[^cite_note-286] In accordance with its immigration policy, Croatia is trying to entice emigrants to return.[^cite_note-287] From 2008 to 2018, Croatia's population dropped by 10%.[^cite_note-288]&#x20;

Historic demographic shifts are closely tied to Croatia's nation-building efforts and the related 1990s [War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_War_of_Independence). The war displaced large numbers of the population and emigration increased. In 1991, in predominantly occupied areas, more than 400,000 Croats were either removed from their homes by [Serb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbs_in_Croatia) forces or fled the violence.[^cite_note-289] During the war's final days, about 150,000–200,000 Serbs fled before the arrival of Croatian forces during [Operation Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Storm).[^cite_note-bbc-storm-121][^cite_note-290] After the war, the number of displaced persons fell to about 250,000.[^cite_note-\:10-291] The Croatian government cared for displaced persons via the social security system and the Office of Displaced Persons and Refugees.[^cite_note-\:10-291] Most of the territories abandoned during the war were settled by Croat refugees from Bosnia and Herzegovina, mostly from north-western Bosnia, while some displaced people returned to their homes.[^cite_note-Index-Cro-Refugees-292][^cite_note-PresidentRH-refugees-293]&#x20;



Religious believers according to the 2011 census



Map of the [Shtokavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shtokavian), [Chakavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chakavian) and [Kajkavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kajkavian) dialects in Croatia by municipality



2011 Croatian population density by county in persons per km

2

According to the 2024 Croatian Bureau of Statistics report, 12.5% to 14.6% of Croatia's population were immigrants.[^cite_note-294] According to the 2021 census, the majority of inhabitants are Croats (91.6%), followed by Serbs (3.2%), [Bosniaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosniaks_of_Croatia) (0.62%), [Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people_in_Croatia) (0.46%), [Albanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanians_of_Croatia) (0.36%), [Italians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italians_of_Croatia) (0.36%), [Hungarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarians_of_Croatia) (0.27%), [Czechs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Czechs_of_Croatia) (0.20%), [Slovenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slovenes_of_Croatia) (0.20%), [Slovaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slovaks_of_Croatia) (0.10%), [Macedonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonians_of_Croatia) (0.09%), [Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germans_of_Croatia) (0.09%), [Montenegrins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montenegrins_of_Croatia) (0.08%), and others (1.56%).[^cite_note-Census_2021-5] Approximately 4 million Croats [live abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_diaspora).[^cite_note-295]&#x20;

### Religion

Main articles: [Religion in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Croatia) and [Catholic Church in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Croatia)

Further information: [List of cathedrals in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cathedrals_in_Croatia) and [List of synagogues in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_synagogues_in_Croatia)



[Šibenik Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%A0ibenik_Cathedral), since 2000 on the [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_List)

Croatia has no official religion.[^cite_note-\:1-298] [Freedom of religion](46006.md) is a constitutional right that protects all [religious communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_denomination) as equal before the law and considers them [separate from the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_church_and_state).[^cite_note-\:1-298]&#x20;

According to the 2021 census, 87.39% of Croatians identify as Christian; of these, Catholics make up the largest group, accounting for 83.04% of the population, after which follows [Eastern Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy) (3.36%), [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) (0.26%), and other Christians (0.10%).[^cite_note-Census2021E-299][^cite_note-Census2021C-300] The largest religion after Christianity is [Islam](6037917.md) (1.32%).[^cite_note-Census2011-religion-301] 4.71% of the population describe itself as non-religious.[^cite_note-Census2011-religion-301] In the [Eurostat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostat) Eurobarometer Poll of 2010, 69% of the population responded that "they believe there is a God".[^cite_note-302] In a 2009 Gallup poll, 70% answered yes to the question "Is religion an important part of your daily life?"[^cite_note-303] Approximately 24% of the population attend religious services regularly.[^cite_note-Pew_Survey_2017_–_Final_Topline-304]&#x20;

### Languages

Main articles: [Croatian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_language), [Gaj's Latin alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaj%27s_Latin_alphabet), [Languages of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Croatia), [Minority languages of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_languages_of_Croatia), and [Bunjevac dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bunjevac_dialect)

See also: [Chakavian dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chakavian_dialect), [Kajkavian dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kajkavian_dialect), and [Shtokavian dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shtokavian_dialect)

Croatian is the official language of the Republic of Croatia and has been an [official language of the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_languages_of_the_European_Union) since Croatia's accession in 2013.[^cite_note-Ustav-RH-305][^cite_note-VL-Hrvatski-EU-306] Croatian replaced [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) as the official language of the Croatian government in the 19th century.[^cite_note-Iadertina-Hr-307] Following the [Vienna Literary Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vienna_Literary_Agreement) in 1850, the language and its Latin alphabet underwent reforms to create an unified "Croatian or Serbian" or "[Serbo-Croatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbo-Croatian)" standard, which under various names became the official language of [Yugoslavia](34244.md).[^cite_note-308] In [SFR Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Federal_Republic_of_Yugoslavia), from 1972 to 1989, the language was constitutionally designated as the "Croatian literary language" and the "Croatian or Serbian language". It was the result of a resistance to and [secession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_secessionism) from "Serbo-Croatian" in the form of the [Declaration on the Status and Name of the Croatian Literary Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_on_the_Status_and_Name_of_the_Croatian_Literary_Language) as part of the [Croatian Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Spring).[^cite_note-309] Since gaining independence in the early 1990s, the Republic of Croatia constitutionally designates the language as "Croatian language" and regulates it through [linguistic prescription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linguistic_prescription). The long-standing aspiration for development of its own expressions and thus enrichment of the language, as opposed to the adoption of foreign solutions in the form of [loanwords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loanwords), has been described by linguists as [Croatian linguistic purism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_linguistic_purism).[^cite_note-310]&#x20;



Location map of dialects in Croatia and areas in Bosnia and Herzegovina with Croat majority. Chakavian in blue.

[Minority languages are in official use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_languages_of_Croatia) in local government units where more than a third of the population consists of national minorities or where local enabling legislation applies. Those languages are [Czech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Czech_language), [Hungarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_language), [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_language_in_Croatia), [Pannonian Rusyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pannonian_Rusyn), [Serbian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_language_in_Croatia), and [Slovak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slovak_language).[^cite_note-311][^cite_note-Franceschini546-312] The following minority languages are also recognised: [Albanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_language), [Bosnian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_language), [Bulgarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_language), [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language), [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language), [Macedonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_language), [Montenegrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montenegrin_language), [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language), [Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_language), [Istro-Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istro-Romanian_language), [Romani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_language), [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language), [Slovene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slovene_language), [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language), [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language), and [Vlach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aromanian_language).[^cite_note-Franceschini546-312]&#x20;

According to the 2021 Census, 95.25% of citizens declared Croatian as their native language, 1.16% declared Serbian as their native language, while no other language reaches 0.26%.[^cite_note-Census-2011-languages-313] Croatian is a member of the [South Slavic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Slavic_languages) and is written using the [Latin alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_alphabet). There are three major dialects spoken on the territory of Croatia, with standard Croatian based on the [Shtokavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shtokavian) dialect. The [Chakavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chakavian) and [Kajkavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kajkavian) dialects are distinguished from Shtokavian by their [lexicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexicon), [phonology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phonology) and [syntax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syntax).[^cite_note-Brozović-HRV-314]&#x20;

A 2011 survey revealed that 78% of Croats claim knowledge of at least one foreign language.[^cite_note-Index-lang-315] According to a 2005 EC survey, 49% of Croats speak English as the second language, 34% speak German, 14% speak Italian, 10% speak French, 4% speak Russian and 2% speak Spanish. However several large municipalities support [minority languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_languages_of_Croatia). A majority of Slovenes (59%) have some knowledge of Croatian.[^cite_note-EBS-243-316] The country is a part of various language-based international associations, most notably the [European Union Language Association.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_the_European_Union)[^cite_note-317]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Croatia)



[National and University Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_and_University_Library_in_Zagreb)



[University of Zagreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Zagreb) is the largest Croatian university and the oldest university in Southeastern Europe.

As of 2024, the literacy rate in Croatia was 99.45%.[^cite_note-318] Primary education in Croatia starts at the age of six or seven and consists of eight grades. In 2007 a law was passed to increase free, noncompulsory education until 18 years of age.[^cite_note-\:11-319] Compulsory education consists of eight grades of elementary school.[^cite_note-\:11-319] Secondary education is provided by [gymnasiums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gymnasium_\(school\)) and vocational schools. As of the 2022–23 school year, there were 2,073 elementary schools and 738 upper secondary schools in Croatia.[^cite_note-320] Primary and secondary education are also available in languages of recognised minorities in Croatia, where classes are held in Czech, Hungarian, Italian, Serbian, German and Slovak languages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia488–489-321]&#x20;

There are 133 elementary and secondary level [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_school) and [art schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_schools),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia486-322] as well as 83 elementary and 44 secondary schools for disabled children and youth[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia484-485-323] and 11 elementary and 52 secondary schools for adults.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia485-324] Nationwide [leaving exams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leaving_exams) ([Croatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_language): *državna matura*) were introduced for secondary education students in the school year 2009–2010. It comprises three compulsory subjects (Croatian language, mathematics, and a foreign language) and optional subjects and is a prerequisite for university education.[^cite_note-MZOS-Matura-325] Croatia has eight public universities and two private universities.[^cite_note-iro-326] The [University of Zadar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Zadar), the first university in Croatia, was founded in 1396 and remained active until 1807, when other institutions of higher education took over until the foundation of the renewed University of Zadar in 2002.[^cite_note-Zadar-Uni-327] The [University of Zagreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Zagreb), founded in 1669, is the oldest continuously operating university in Southeast Europe.[^cite_note-Zagreb-Uni-328] There are also 15 [polytechnics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_technology), of which two are private, and 30 higher education institutions, of which 27 are private.[^cite_note-iro-326] In total, there are 131 institutions of higher education in Croatia, attended by more than 160 thousand students.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia490-329]&#x20;

In 2022, Croatia's [research and development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_development) (R\&D) expenditure was approximately 1.43% of GDP. Among the scientific institutes operating in Croatia, the largest is the [Ruđer Bošković Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ru%C4%91er_Bo%C5%A1kovi%C4%87_Institute) in Zagreb.[^cite_note-330] The [Croatian Academy of Sciences and Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Academy_of_Sciences_and_Arts) in Zagreb is a [learned society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Learned_society) promoting language, culture, arts and science from its inception in 1866.[^cite_note-HAZU-founding-331] Croatia was ranked 40th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333]&#x20;

The [European Investment Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Investment_Bank) provided digital infrastructure and equipment to around 150 primary and secondary schools in Croatia. Twenty of these schools got specialised assistance in the form of gear, software, and services to help them integrate the teaching and administrative operations.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335] In 2024, the EIB extended a €207 million loan to the [City of Zagreb](55906.md) for infrastructure upgrades, including investments in renewable energy, energy efficiency projects, social and affordable housing, schools, and kindergartens.[^cite_note-European_Investment_Bank-336][^cite_note-European_Investment_Bank_2-337] Additionally, the EIB signed a €49 million loan with the [City of Split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split,_Croatia) to co-finance its 2023–2027 multi-sector investment programme, which includes refurbishing public buildings, increasing energy efficiency, and improving technical equipment for public information and communication.[^cite_note-European_Investment_Bank-336][^cite_note-European_Investment_Bank_2-337]&#x20;

### Healthcare

Main article: [Healthcare in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Croatia)



[University Hospital Centre Zagreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_Hospital_Centre_Zagreb) is the largest hospital in Croatia and the teaching hospital of the [University of Zagreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Zagreb).

Croatia has a [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care) system, whose roots can be traced back to the Hungarian-Croatian Parliament Act of 1891, providing a form of mandatory insurance of all factory workers and craftsmen.[^cite_note-RSP-1891-338] The population is covered by a basic health insurance plan provided by statute and optional insurance. In 2017, annual healthcare related expenditures reached 22.2 billion kuna (around €3.0 billion).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia546-339] Healthcare expenditures make up only 0.6% of private health insurance and public spending.[^cite_note-Vjesnik-Zdravstvo-340] In 2022, Croatia spent around 7.2% of its GDP on healthcare.[^cite_note-341] In 2025, Croatia ranked 53rd in the world in life expectancy with 75.8 years for men and 81.9 years for women, and it had a low [infant mortality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality_rate) of 3.3 per 1,000 [live births](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Live_birth_\(human\)).[^cite_note-342]&#x20;

There are hundreds of healthcare institutions in Croatia, including 75 hospitals, and 13 clinics with 23,049 beds. The hospitals and clinics care for more than 700 thousand patients per year and employ 6,642 [medical doctors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_doctors), including 4,773 specialists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia525-343] There is a total of 69,841 health workers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia524-344] There are 119 emergency units in health centres, responding to more than a million calls.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia532-345] The principal cause of death in 2022 was [cardiovascular disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_disease) at 34.3% for men and 43.8% for women, followed by [hypertensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypertension) disease.[^cite_note-346] In 2022 it was estimated that 37% of Croatians are smokers.[^cite_note-347] According to 2022 data, 35.65% of the Croatian adult population is obese.[^cite_note-348]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Croatia)

Further information: [World Heritage Sites in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Sites_in_Croatia), [Register of Cultural Goods of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Register_of_Cultural_Goods_of_Croatia), and [List of museums in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_museums_in_Croatia)



The historic centre of [Trogir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trogir) has been included in the [UNESCO](21786641.md) list of [World Heritage Site](44940.md) since 1997.

[^cite_note-349]



[Pula Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pula_Arena), Roman amphitheatre located in [Pula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pula), constructed between 27 BC and AD 68.

Because of its geographical position, Croatia represents a blend of four different cultural spheres. It has been a crossroads of influences from western culture and the east since the schism between the [Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire) and the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire), and also from Central Europe and [Mediterranean culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_culture).[^cite_note-HTZ-History-Culture-350] The [Illyrian movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illyrian_movement) was the most significant period of national cultural history, as the 19th century proved crucial to the emancipation of Croatians and saw unprecedented developments in all fields of art and culture, giving rise to many [historical figures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_from_Croatia).[^cite_note-CRIS-Stančić-59]&#x20;

The [Ministry of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Culture_\(Croatia\)) is tasked with preserving the nation's [cultural and natural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_and_Cultural_Heritage_of_Croatia) and overseeing its development. Further activities supporting the development of culture are undertaken at the local government level.[^cite_note-MKult-Djelokrug-351] The [UNESCO](21786641.md)'s [World Heritage List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Sites_by_country) includes [ten sites in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ten_sites_in_Croatia) and a list of Intangible Cultural Heritage of Croatia.[^cite_note-352] The country is rich with [intangible culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intangible_culture) and holds 15 of [UNESCO's World's intangible culture masterpieces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO%27s_World%27s_intangible_culture_masterpieces), ranking fourth in the world.[^cite_note-353] A global cultural contribution from Croatia is the necktie, derived from the [cravat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cravat_\(early\)) originally worn by the 17th-century Croatian mercenaries in France.[^cite_note-NYT-tie-354][^cite_note-Huzjan-355]&#x20;

In 2019, Croatia had 95 professional theatres, 30 professional children's theatres, and 51 amateur theatres visited by more than 2.27 million viewers per year. Professional theatres employ 1,195 artists. There are 42 professional orchestras, ensembles, and choirs, attracting an annual attendance of 297 thousand. There are 75 cinemas with 166 screens and attendance of 5.026 million.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia512–513-356]&#x20;

Croatia has 222 museums, visited by more than 2.71 million people in 2023.[^cite_note-357] Furthermore, there are 1,768 libraries, containing 26.8 million volumes, and 19 state archives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia516–521-358] The book publishing market is dominated by several major publishers and the industry's centrepiece event—[Interliber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interliber) exhibition held annually at [Zagreb Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagreb_Fair).[^cite_note-JL-Interliber-359]&#x20;

### Arts, literature, and music

Main articles: [Croatian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_art), [Architecture of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Croatia), [Croatian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_literature), and [Croatian music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_music)



Historical nucleus of [Split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split,_Croatia) with the 4th-century [Diocletian's Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocletian%27s_Palace) was inscribed on the [UNESCO](21786641.md) list of [World Heritage Sites](44940.md) in 1979.



[Euphrasian Basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphrasian_Basilica) in [Poreč](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pore%C4%8D), example of early [Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture), on the [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_List) since 1997

Architecture in Croatia reflects influences of bordering nations. Austrian and Hungarian influence is visible in public spaces and buildings in the north and the central regions, architecture found along coasts of Dalmatia and Istria exhibits Venetian influence.[^cite_note-360] Squares named after culture heroes, parks, and pedestrian-only zones, are features of Croatian towns and cities, especially where large scale [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_architecture) urban planning took place, for instance in Osijek ([Tvrđa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tvr%C4%91a)), Varaždin, and Karlovac.[^cite_note-Telegraph-Baroque-361][^cite_note-JL-Karlovac-Baroque-362] The subsequent influence of the [Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau) was reflected in contemporary architecture.[^cite_note-IPU-Art-Nouveau-363] The architecture is the Mediterranean with a Venetian and Renaissance influence in major coastal urban areas exemplified in works of [Giorgio da Sebenico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giorgio_da_Sebenico) and Nicolas of Florence such as the [Cathedral of St. James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%A0ibenik_Cathedral) in Šibenik. The oldest preserved examples of Croatian architecture are the 9th-century churches, with the largest and the most representative among them being [Church of St. Donatus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_St._Donatus) in [Zadar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zadar).[^cite_note-MVPEI-Art-364][^cite_note-TZZadar-Donat-365]&#x20;



[Marko Marulić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marko_Maruli%C4%87) (18 August 1450 – 5 January 1524), Croatian poet, lawyer, judge, and Renaissance humanist who coined the term "psychology". He is the [national poet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_poet) of Croatia.

Besides the architecture encompassing the oldest artworks, there is a history of artists in Croatia reaching the Middle Ages. In that period the stone portal of the [Trogir Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trogir_Cathedral) was made by [Radovan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radovan_\(master\)), representing the most important monument of [Romanesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanesque_art) sculpture from [Medieval Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatia_in_the_union_with_Hungary). The [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_in_Croatia) had the greatest impact on the Adriatic Sea coast since the remainder was embroiled in the Hundred Years' Croatian–Ottoman War. With the waning of the Ottoman Empire, art flourished during the [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque) and [Rococo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rococo). The 19th and 20th centuries brought affirmation of numerous Croatian artisans, helped by several patrons of the arts such as bishop [Josip Juraj Strossmayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josip_Juraj_Strossmayer).[^cite_note-Essehist-Strossmayer-366] Croatian artists of the period achieving renown were [Vlaho Bukovac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlaho_Bukovac), [Ivan Meštrović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Me%C5%A1trovi%C4%87), and [Ivan Generalić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Generali%C4%87).[^cite_note-MVPEI-Art-364][^cite_note-Hintz_2004-367]&#x20;

The Baška tablet, a stone inscribed with the [glagolitic alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glagolitic_alphabet) found on the Krk island and dated to c. 1100, is considered to be the oldest surviving prose in Croatian.[^cite_note-KRK-Baška-368] The beginning of more vigorous development of Croatian literature is marked by the Renaissance and [Marko Marulić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marko_Maruli%C4%87). Besides Marulić, Renaissance playwright [Marin Držić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marin_Dr%C5%BEi%C4%87), Baroque poet [Ivan Gundulić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Gunduli%C4%87), [Croatian national revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_national_revival) poet [Ivan Mažuranić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Ma%C5%BEurani%C4%87), novelist, playwright, and poet [August Šenoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_%C5%A0enoa), children's writer [Ivana Brlić-Mažuranić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivana_Brli%C4%87-Ma%C5%BEurani%C4%87), writer and journalist [Marija Jurić Zagorka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marija_Juri%C4%87_Zagorka), poet and writer [Antun Gustav Matoš](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antun_Gustav_Mato%C5%A1), poet [Antun Branko Šimić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antun_Branko_%C5%A0imi%C4%87), [expressionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expressionist) and [realist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(arts\)) writer [Miroslav Krleža](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miroslav_Krle%C5%BEa), poet [Tin Ujević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin_Ujevi%C4%87) and novelist, and short story writer [Ivo Andrić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivo_Andri%C4%87) are often cited as the greatest figures in Croatian literature.[^cite_note-LZMK-369][^cite_note-NYT-Readerguide-370]&#x20;

Croatian music varies from classical operas to modern-day rock. [Vatroslav Lisinski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vatroslav_Lisinski) created the country's first opera, *Love and Malice*, in 1846. [Ivan Zajc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Zajc) composed more than a thousand pieces of music, including masses and oratorios. Pianist [Ivo Pogorelić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivo_Pogoreli%C4%87) has performed across the world.[^cite_note-Hintz_2004-367]&#x20;

### Media

Main articles: [Media of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Croatia) and [List of radio stations in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_radio_stations_in_Croatia)

In Croatia, the Constitution guarantees the freedom of the press and the freedom of speech.[^cite_note-worldpress-371] Croatia ranked 64th in the 2019 [Press Freedom Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index) report compiled by [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md) which noted that journalists who investigate corruption, organised crime or war crimes face challenges and that the Government was trying to influence the public broadcaster [HRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Radiotelevision)'s editorial policies.[^cite_note-index-372] In its 2019 Freedom in the World report, the [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) classified freedoms of press and speech in Croatia as generally free from political interference and manipulation, noting that journalists still face threats and occasional attacks.[^cite_note-373] The state-owned news agency [HINA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HINA) runs a [wire service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wire_service) in Croatian and English on politics, economics, society, and culture.[^cite_note-HINA-about-374]&#x20;



[Radio Zagreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Radio), now a part of [Croatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_language) national [public broadcasting](178808.md) company, [Croatian Radiotelevision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Radiotelevision), was the first public radio station in Southeast Europe.

[^cite_note-375]

As of January 2021, there are thirteen nationwide free-to-air [DVB-T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DVB-T) television channels, with [Croatian Radiotelevision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Radiotelevision) (HRT) operating four, [RTL Televizija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RTL_\(Croatian_TV_channel\)) three, and [Nova TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_TV_\(Croatia\)) operating two channels, and the [Croatian Olympic Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Olympic_Committee), Kapital Net d.o.o., and Author d.o.o. companies operate the remaining three.[^cite_note-376] Also, there are 21 regional or local DVB-T television channels.[^cite_note-OIV-DVBT-377] The HRT is also broadcasting a satellite TV channel.[^cite_note-HRT-Sat-378] In 2020, there were 147 radio stations and 27 TV stations in Croatia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia510-379][^cite_note-380] Cable television and [IPTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPTV) networks are gaining ground. Cable television already serves 450 thousand people, around 10% of the total population of the country.[^cite_note-Lider-IPTV-381][^cite_note-JL-cableTV-382]&#x20;

In 2010, 267 newspapers and 2,676 magazines were published in Croatia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia510-379] The print media market is dominated by the Croatian-owned [Hanza Media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanza_Media) and Austrian-owned [Styria Media Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Styria_Media_Group) who publish their flagship dailies *[Jutarnji list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jutarnji_list)*, *[Večernji list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ve%C4%8Dernji_list)* and *[24sata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/24sata_\(Croatia\))*. Other influential newspapers are *[Novi list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novi_list)* and *[Slobodna Dalmacija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slobodna_Dalmacija)*.[^cite_note-EPH-Print-383][^cite_note-Styria-dailies-384] In 2020, *24sata* was the most widely circulated daily newspaper, followed by *Večernji list* and *Jutarnji list*.[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386]&#x20;

[Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatia_in_the_Eurovision_Song_Contest) competed in the [Eurovision Song Contest](9954.md) as part of [Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslavia_in_the_Eurovision_Song_Contest) since 1961. The first and only victory Yugoslavia achieved in the competition was accomplished by the Croatian pop band [Riva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riva_\(band\)) in 1989. Since its debut at the [1993 contest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_1993), Croatia won two fourth places at the [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_1996) and [1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_1999) contests, and one second place at the [2024 contest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_2024), marking the country's best result to date as an independent nation.[^cite_note-387]&#x20;

### Film industry

Main articles: [Cinema of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Croatia) and [Television in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Croatia)

Croatia's film industry is small and heavily subsidised by the government, mainly through grants approved by the Ministry of Culture with films often being co-produced by HRT.[^cite_note-JL-Film-388][^cite_note-HRT-coproducing-389] Croatian cinema produces between five and ten feature films per year.[^cite_note-390] [Pula Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pula_Film_Festival), the national film awards event held annually in [Pula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pula), is the most prestigious film event featuring national and international productions.[^cite_note-Vjesnik-PulaFilm-391] [Animafest Zagreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animafest_Zagreb), founded in 1972, is the prestigious annual film festival dedicated to the animated film. The first greatest accomplishment by Croatian filmmakers was achieved by [Dušan Vukotić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Du%C5%A1an_Vukoti%C4%87) when he won the 1961 [Academy Award for Best Animated Short Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Animated_Short_Film) for *[Ersatz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surogat)* ([Croatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_language): *Surogat*).[^cite_note-Vjesnik-surogat-392] Croatian film producer [Branko Lustig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Branko_Lustig) won the [Academy Awards for Best Picture](61702.md) for *[Schindler's List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schindler%27s_List)* and *[Gladiator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gladiator_\(2000_film\))*.[^cite_note-Film_Producer_Branko_Lustig_Becomes_Honorary_Citizen_of_Zagreb-393] In addition to that, Croatian filmmaker [Nebojša Slijepčević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neboj%C5%A1a_Slijep%C4%8Devi%C4%87) got nominated for [97th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/97th_Academy_Awards) in category for [Best Live Action Short Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Live_Action_Short_Film) for his 2024 movie *[The Man Who Could Not Remain Silent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Man_Who_Could_Not_Remain_Silent)* ([Croatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_language): *Čovjek koji nije mogao šutjeti*), making it first Croatian nomination in that category, and first since its [independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_independence).[^cite_note-394]&#x20;

Before and since its [independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Croatia), Croatia has become a popular filming destination amongs international filming productions, and a lot of [blockbuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockbuster_\(entertainment\)) films and TV series have been filmed in Croatia including: *[Game of Thrones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Game_of_Thrones)*, *[Star Wars: The Last Jedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_Wars:_The_Last_Jedi)*, *[Robin Hood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Hood_\(2018_film\))* in Dubrovnik, *[Speak No Evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speak_No_Evil_\(2024_film\))*, *[Season of the Witch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Season_of_the_Witch_\(2011_film\))* and *[Titius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titus_\(film\))* in Istria, *[Infinity Pool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infinity_Pool_\(film\))* in Šibenik, *[Canary Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canary_Black)*, *[Hitman's Wife's Bodyguard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitman%27s_Wife%27s_Bodyguard)*, *[Sophie's Choice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sophie%27s_Choice_\(film\))*, *[Citizen Vigilante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizen_Vigilante)*, *[Armour of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armour_of_God_\(film\))* and *[Fiddler on the roof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiddler_on_the_Roof_\(film\))* in Zagreb, *[Mamma Mia! Here We Go Again](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamma_Mia!_Here_We_Go_Again)* on island of Vis, *[Succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_\(TV_series\))* on the island of Korčula, *[Hercules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hercules_\(2014_film\))*, *[The Weekend Away](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Weekend_Away)*, *[Bliss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bliss_\(2021_film\))* in Split, *[The Peacemaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Peacemaker_\(1997_film\))*, *[Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_\(2023_film\))* and many others.[^cite_note-395][^cite_note-396] Croatia became an international filming location thanks to its biodiversity, natural landscape and cheaper filming costs.[^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398] In last 11 years there has been 122 projects for international films in Croatia, and €263 million spent as part of the Filming in Croatia which increased in last years due to high demand for its location.[^cite_note-399]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main articles: [Croatian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_cuisine), [Croatian wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_wine), and [Beer in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_in_Croatia)

See also: [List of Croatian dishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Croatian_dishes)



[Zagorski štrukli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagorski_%C5%A1trukli)

Croatian traditional cuisine varies from one region to another. Dalmatia and Istria have culinary influences of [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_cuisine) and other [Mediterranean cuisines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_cuisine) which prominently feature various seafood, cooked vegetables and pasta, and condiments such as olive oil and garlic. [Austrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_cuisine), [Hungarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_cuisine), [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_cuisine), and [Balkan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_cuisine) culinary styles influenced continental cuisine. In that area, meats, freshwater fish, and vegetable dishes are predominant.[^cite_note-HTZ-cuisine-400]&#x20;

There are two distinct wine-producing regions in Croatia. The continental in the northeast of the country, especially Slavonia, produces premium wines, particularly whites. Along the north coast, Istrian and Krk wines are similar to those in neighbouring Italy, while further south in Dalmatia, Mediterranean-style red wines are the norm.[^cite_note-HTZ-cuisine-400] Annual production of wine exceeds 72 million litres as of 2017.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2018_Statistical_Yearbook_of_the_Republic_of_Croatia265-401] Croatia was almost exclusively a wine-consuming country up until the late 18th century when a more massive beer production and consumption started.[^cite_note-skenderovic-402]&#x20;

As of 2026, 111 restaurants in Croatia are featured on the [Michelin Guide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin_Guide), with 14 of them having at least one Michelin star.[^cite_note-403]&#x20;

### Sports

Main articles: [Sport in Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Croatia) and [Croatia at the Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatia_at_the_Olympics)



[Croatia national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatia_national_football_team) in [World Cup 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Cup_2018) in Russia

Croatia has a long tradition of sports dating back to Roman times, followed by popular medieval [knights' tournaments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tournament_\(medieval\)).[^cite_note-b918-404] Modern organized sports began in the late 19th century with the founding of sports associations like Hrvatski Sokol in 1874.[^cite_note-b918-404] By the early 20th century, many sports organizations emerged, including the [Croatian Sports Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Sports_Association) established in 1909.[^cite_note-Ekon-405] Croatian sports' development has been closely related to the Olympic movement, with [Franjo Bučar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franjo_Bu%C4%8Dar) playing a key role in promoting sports for [Croatian independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Croatia).[^cite_note-Ekon-405] Sport is part of the school curriculum, and many children engage in extracurricular activities.[^cite_note-x676-406] To support sports development, Croatia enacted the National Sport Programme in 2019.[^cite_note-j068-407] Funding for sports comes from the state budget, sponsorship, and membership fees.[^cite_note-v584-408] The [Croatian Olympic Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Olympic_Committee), established in 1991, oversees over 80 national sports associations.[^cite_note-f065-409]&#x20;

The most popular sports in Croatia are football, handball, basketball and water polo.[^cite_note-b918-404] In 2024, there were over 4,500 chess association members.[^cite_note-410] With about 12,500 sports associations, [football is the most popular sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Croatia), hosting nearly 1,500 clubs and 110,000 players, achieving significant milestones, including bronze at the [1998 World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_World_Cup), silver at the [2018 World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_World_Cup) and another bronze at [2022 World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_World_Cup).[^cite_note-b918-404] [Croatians have participated in the Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatia_at_the_Olympics) since 1992 and have won [59 Olympic medals, including 20 gold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatia_at_the_Olympics#Medals).[^cite_note-y175-411] The [national tennis team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatia_Davis_Cup_team) has won two [Davis Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davis_Cup) titles and won a gold medal at the [2020 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennis_at_the_2020_Summer_Olympics) in men's doubles.[^cite_note-c001-412] The [national handball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatia_men%27s_national_handball_team) and [water polo teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatia_men%27s_national_water_polo_team) are also been successful, each achieving multiple championships and medals.[^cite_note-x798-413] [Rowing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Rowing_Federation) has seen success as well, winning multiple Olympic and World Championship medals.[^cite_note-d368-414] In gymnastics, athletes have also made their mark, winning medals in European and World Championships.[^cite_note-h046-415]&#x20;

Croatia hosted several major sports competitions, including the [2009 World Men's Handball Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_World_Men%27s_Handball_Championship), the [2007 World Table Tennis Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_World_Table_Tennis_Championships), the [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010) [European Individual Chess Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Individual_Chess_Championship), the [2000 World Rowing Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_World_Rowing_Championships), the [1987 Summer Universiade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_Summer_Universiade), the [1979 Mediterranean Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Mediterranean_Games), and several [European Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Championship), including the [2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_European_Men%27s_Handball_Championship), [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_European_Men%27s_Handball_Championship) and [2025 World Men's Handball Championship](62967643.md), [2024 Men's European Water Polo Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Men%27s_European_Water_Polo_Championship).&#x20;

[^cite_note-188]: &#x20;["Statement by the Presidency of the Croatian Academy of Sciences and Arts regarding the Bunjevci Croats"](https://www.info.hazu.hr/wp-content/uploads/2021/02/Glasnik-Broj-2.pdf) (PDF). *info.hazu.hr*. Glasnik HAZU. 2014. p. 53. The Presidency of the Croatian Academy of Sciences and Arts, in a session held on 12 September 2014, made the following statement explaining that the Bunjevci Croats form an integral part of the Croatian national corpus. The Presidency of the Croatian Academy of Sciences and Arts, one of the fundamental institutions of the Croatian nation and of all the citizens of the Republic of Croatia, among the roles of which belongs the preservation of national identity, made the following statement in a session held on 12 September 2014: The Bunjevci, a Croatian ethnic group, are made up of three branches: the Dalmatian-Herzegovinian branch; the Primorje-Lika branch; and the Danube Region branch. Not encroaching on the right of any individual to express their national affiliation based on their origin, history, traditional culture, customs and language – the western new-Štokavian and Ikavian – the Bunjevci Croats form an integral part of the Croatian national corpus.

Since its independence in 1991 and its transformation into a market economy, Croatia has led the way for regional peers with a reform agenda that paved the way for its EU accession in 2013. Croatia also plays a central role in the OECD's South East Europe Regional Programme, engaging in project collaboration and sharing policies and good practices in the regional policy dialogue and peer learning activities. Croatia co-organises the OECD Dubrovnik Talks as part of its annual Dubrovnik Forum, playing a leading role advancing social, economic and environmental reforms in South East Europe.
